SVM ^gSNDsDoQ

A Support Vector Machine (SVM) is a supervised machine-learning algorithm primarily used for classification, although it can also perform regression. ^8FkSkc2f

plane
hyperplane ^3ivBNUiq

decision boundry ^qmvawijR

At its core, SVM tries to solve a simple problem: ^dy6TOLSL

Find the best possible boundary that separates different classes of data. ^Qzb8trNh

A generic classifier might simply ask: ^bD9KQR7f

"Where should I put my decision boundary?" ^dGDzlsrs

SVM asks something more specific: ^hKQsrFDR

"Where should I put the boundary so that I maximize the distance between it and the closest observations from both classes?" ^CxbGIx0T

Hyperplane = the geometric form of a linear SVM's decision boundary. ^xKwPH2IP

A hyperplane is a flat decision boundary that has one dimension less than the space in which the data exists. ^tfCXOpTu

In real-world data, it is common for classes to overlap, leading to classification errors.

To address this, SVC introduces the concept of soft margin, allowing some misclassification or overlap in the data to achieve a better generalization for unseen data.
On the other hand, a hard margin exists when the data is perfectly separable, without any errors, which is less common in practical applications. ^srxFaUry

This balance between maximizing the margin and allowing for some errors is what makes SVC powerful in handling complex classification tasks. ^io8xr6MZ

Bias: The inability of a model to truly capture the relationships in the training data. ^jdtVBDoS

Bias is the error caused by a model making overly simple assumptions about the relationship between features and the target. ^UDaKkTg6

The model simply doesn't have enough flexibility to represent the real pattern. ^2wTEYQfu

High Bias → Underfitting ^biaBObul

For example, imagine: ^AlW9gm18

Training accuracy = 65% ^yiSgsgnp

Test accuracy     = 63% ^KYocOdYQ

A high-bias model typically:
- performs poorly on the training data
- also performs poorly on unseen/test data
- is too simple for the underlying relationship ^zAATfSUQ

Plot of the second-order polynomial with noise ^y3EUR6nQ

Plot of the second-order polynomial after splitting into train and test data ^TE2Q5AmB

Underfitting is also termed as “bias”. 

-> An underfit model doesn’t align with many points in the training data and carries on its own path.
-> It doesn’t allow the data points to modify itself much.
->An underfit model, or a model with high bias, is simplistic and can’t capture much essence or inherent information in the training data.
-> Thus, it can not generalize well to the testing (unseen) data. Both the training and testing accuracies of such a model are low. ^6x4r7ZVm

Overfitting is referred to as “variance”.
-> In this case, the model aligns itself with most, if not all, data points in the training set.

An overfit model, or a model with a high variance, tries to capture every minute detail of the training data, including the noise, so much so that it can’t generalize to the testing (unseen) data.

In the case of a model with an overfit, the training accuracy is high, but the testing accuracy is low. ^iPzurqWY
